# GLOBAL CREDIT TRADER

# The Hybrid Renaissance

The momentum behind hybrid debt issuance—which began in early 2024 following a change to Moody’s rating methodology—has accelerated in 2026. While the elevated pace of year-to-date hybrid supply is especially notable in the EUR market, the USD market is also on track to set a new annual record.   
The amount of outstanding USD hybrid debt has doubled vs. early 2024. Even so, hybrid spreads have outperformed both BBBs and BBs since that time. The recent performance of EUR hybrids vs. BBBs has been more range-bound, owing to the more mature stage of the market (and significant outperformance in 2022).   
For IG-focused investors searching for incremental spread (vs. BBBs), hybrids can be an attractive solution, as they offer a spread premium to compensate for structural subordination—typically within an IG-rated parent company capital structure—and certain issuer-friendly terms. That said, determining whether this compensation is adequate requires a clear understanding of issuer-specific capital management priorities and sector-specific headwinds.   
- USD hybrids offer (on average) 61bp of additional spread vs. BBBs. We view this as moderately attractive in the context of the tight spread environment and expect the spread premium to compress vs. BBBs as the hybrid market grows further.   
In the EUR market, the incremental spread ‘pickup’ of hybrids vs. BBBs is 53bp. We do not see much scope for further absolute spread compression, given the more challenging growth-inflation-monetary policy mix in the region. That said, the additional carry of hybrids vs. EUR BBBs can be attractive for a range of investor types. Given our more cautious positioning in the EUR market (relative to USD), we would focus on higher-quality hybrid issuers in more economically resilient sectors.

# The Hybrid Renaissance

# Hybrid supply: On pace to set new records, especially in the EUR market

The issuance tailwind behind the hybrid debt market—which began in early 2024 following a change to the Moody’s rating methodology—has accelerated in 2026. As shown in Exhibit 1, both the USD and EUR markets are on pace to set new annual records for hybrid gross supply.

In the USD market, there are now more than a dozen hybrid bonds with \$1 billion or more of notional outstanding—and some of the largest hybrid new issues were launched in recent months. The elevated gross supply of EUR hybrids partly reflects a more mature market, which has had a high share of bonds being called this year. For comparison, while gross supply is up 132% year to date vs. the same period last year, the notional amount outstanding of the EUR hybrid market has increased by a more modest 9% since the end of 2025.

Utilities have been among the most active sectors in both markets, accounting for 50% and 40% of hybrid supply in the USD and EUR markets, respectively, since 2024 (Exhibits 2 and 3).

# The drivers of hybrid issuance and investment

With the broader debt capital markets open to a wide range of borrowers, many market participants have questioned why an issuer might opt for a subordinated (higher coupon) hybrid instrument vs. a more traditional financing option in senior unsecured form.

Issuers in capital intensive industries—which often operate with large debt capital structures—tend to favor hybrid debt for two key reasons. First is the partial equity credit it receives from the rating agencies, which reflects its ‘equity like’ characteristics, including subordination in the capital structure and the ability to defer coupon payments without triggering a default. For a capital structure with limited ‘headroom’ within a targeted leverage or rating band, this partial equity credit can be attractive—especially since it does not involve diluting shareholders.

Second is the favorable tax deductibility of hybrid interest (relative to preferred stock, for example). For a profitable corporate taxpayer, the tax shield can help optimize the weighted average cost of capital—even if the hybrid debt has a moderately higher coupon.

# Placing the growth of the hybrid market (across regions) in context

Exhibits 4 and 5 show the evolution of the size of the hybrid asset class across regions. We start in the USD market and first turn to the ICE-BAML USD Developed Markets Corporate (ex-Banks) Hybrid Bond Index as a proxy for the USD hybrid universe.

As shown in Exhibit 4, the USD Hybrid Index had \$140 billion of notional outstanding as of June 3, 2026—up 96% vs. January 2024. The number of bonds in the index has also doubled since that time (to 172, currently). For context, the notional size of the ICE-BAML USD BBB Corporate Index grew 5% (to \$4.3 trillion) over that same time frame and the BB Corporate Index grew 41% (to \$880 billion). Of note, these BBB and BB indices include hybrids.

# Assessing relative value—with some important caveats

It is important to acknowledge up front that the composition of these EUR and USD hybrid indices can make direct relative value comparisons to the broader corporate credit market challenging. For example, in both regions, the hybrid indices contain a mix of ratings across IG and HY (for example, blending BBBs and BBs together, in one index), and fewer—yet more concentrated—industry exposures.

Exhibits 6 through 9 provide snapshot summaries of the USD and EUR hybrid indices by rating and by industry. Given the rating distributions shown in Exhibits 6 and 8, we view BBB rated debt (and, to a lesser extent, BBs) as the most relevant comparable to hybrids. Additionally, the concentrated hybrid sector weights shown in Exhibits 7 and 9 are important to keep in mind when interpreting the index-level relative value relationships that follow.

# Performance: Solid (despite recent growth) for USD hybrids and range-bound for EUR

The swift growth in the USD hybrid market has not derailed performance. As Exhibit 10 illustrates, the spread ratio of the USD Hybrid Index vs. both BBBs and BBs has compressed since early 2024—indicating hybrid outperformance.

In the EUR market, the recent performance backdrop for hybrids has been largely range-bound—at least compared to BBBs (Exhibit 11). This likely reflects, in our view, the more mature stage of the market and the significant periods of outperformance in prior years (most recently in 2022, for example).

The more mature state of the EUR hybrid market is also a key reason why we show a longer performance history in Exhibit 11 relative to the shorter performance history for USD hybrids in Exhibit 10. As highlighted earlier in Exhibit 4, the USD hybrid market did not cross \$90 billion in notional value until late 2024. Meanwhile, the EUR hybrid market stood at €95 billion in notional outstanding as early as 2015 (again, Exhibit 5).

Exhibits 12 and 13 provide a comparison of excess returns, which remove the performance influence from interest rates and focus instead on credit risk. The takeaway is clear, in our view: in periods where excess returns are positive, hybrids tend to outperform their BBB peers (albeit to varying degrees across markets and periods). However, in a year such as 2022 when excess returns were negative, hybrids underperformed.

# Hybrids generally trade structure risk for quality risk

For IG-focused investors searching for ways to capture incremental spread without deviating too far down the quality spectrum, hybrids can be an attractive solution given the spread premium they offer vs. senior debt.

This additional spread premia serves as ‘compensation’ for hybrids’ subordination as well as some issuer-friendly structural features, such as the ability to defer interest payments in certain situations (without triggering a default).

That said, determining whether this compensation is adequate requires a clear understanding of the company-specific capital management priorities for each hybrid issuer. It also requires a thorough analysis of the potential long-term fundamental headwinds facing the issuers' industries to evaluate the risk of financial distress (which may necessitate deferral of interest).

Very few investors expect hybrid issuers to actually utilize the provision to defer interest—especially if that issuer already has a large debt capital structure which requires frequent refinancing. The assumption is that deferring the hybrid’s coupon payments may make it more difficult—due to investor push back from overlapping buyer bases—to refinance the ‘regular’ (non-subordinated) debt when the time comes. In many instances, deferring a hybrid coupon may also prevent an issuer from paying its common equity dividend.

The value of this interest deferral provision to the issuer increases during times of economic weakness, which can often result in hybrids underperforming during risk-off moves (again, Exhibit 12 and 13).

# Quantifying the compensation for subordination

To quantify this compensation premium, we again use the USD and EUR hybrid indices mentioned earlier, comparing index-level average (and current) spreads relative to the broader BBB and BB markets. Leaving the important rating and industry compositional difference caveats aside, we show the index-level absolute spread differentials across regions in Exhibit 14 and 15.

Source: ICE, GS Global Investment Research

For example, the absolute spread differential between USD hybrids and USD BBBs compressed from 99bp (on average) in 2023 to 61bp, currently (again, Exhibit 14). While this is narrower vs. a few years ago, it is consistent with the directional trend of spread tightening in the USD market. As a result, we view this index-level compensation as moderately attractive considering spreads for many markets are hovering at the tight end of the post-financial crisis history (Exhibits 37 and 38 show spread percentile ranks vs. history). We also expect the hybrid spread premium to compress as the market grows further.

In the EUR market, the hybrid vs. BBB spread differential is currently 53bp (again, Exhibit 15). This tighter spread differential likely reflects, in part, the more mature positioning of the EUR hybrid market, as investors and issuers have become very familiar with the structures over the past decade.

We do not see much scope for further absolute spread tightening (compression) in EUR hybrids, given the more challenging growth-inflation-monetary policy mix stemming from the ongoing energy shock. That said, the additional carry vs. EUR BBBs can be attractive for a range of investor types. Given our more cautious positioning in the EUR market (relative to USD), we would focus on higher-quality hybrid issuers in more economically resilient sectors.

The authors would like to thank Patricia Pacheco for her contribution to this report.

# Forecast dashboard: Relative value views

Exhibit 20: Relative value views 

<table><tr><td colspan="3">Relative value views</td></tr><tr><td colspan="3">Corporate Credit</td></tr><tr><td>Regional</td><td>Overweight USD vs. EUR</td><td>The growth, inflation and monetary policy mix is somewhat more favorable in the US, relative to Europe. As a result, and relative to the USD market, we forecast a wider peak (and a slower recovery in 2H2026) in EUR credit spreads.</td></tr><tr><td rowspan="7">USD market</td><td>Overweight IG vs. HY</td><td>We have a slight preference for IG vs. HY in the USD market, owing to valuations and our economists&#x27; forecasts for below-potential growth. We view the BB/BBB spread ratio as fairly priced amid a somewhat more challenging growth and inflation mix, and have a neutral view between the two rating cohorts.</td></tr><tr><td>Overweight BBBs vs. higher-rated cohorts</td><td>We prefer owning BBBs over the higher-rated segments of USD IG (As and AAs), as a way to capture additional spread. We also see scope for balance sheet deterioration at the highest end of the rating spectrum in IG.</td></tr><tr><td>Overweight BBs, Neutral CCCs, Underweight Bs</td><td>Current spread levels indicate that single-Bs offer the lowest credit risk premium relative to expected losses among all HY rating buckets.</td></tr><tr><td>Overweight HY bonds vs. loans</td><td>HY bonds still provide a superior trade-off between carry and duration compared to leveraged loans. In addition, loans&#x27; substantial software sector exposure is likely to present a sentiment overhang, at least in the near-term.</td></tr><tr><td>Overweight CLO mezz tranches vs. leveraged loansNeutral CLO vs. USD IGNeutral CLO vs. CMOs</td><td>For floating rate investors, we prefer CLO mezzanine tranches vs. leveraged loans. Historical data points to low impairment rates for BB and BBB CLO tranches, and current valuations offer attractive carry and a cushion against moderate to elevated default outcomes. Elsewhere, we are neutral between senior CLO tranches and USD IG; we would use spread widening to add exposure in CLOs. We are also neutral between CLO AAA and CMO floaters.</td></tr><tr><td>Prefer Agency MBS vs. IG</td><td>Agency MBS offers an attractive diversification benefit vs. a portfolio of IG corporate bonds.</td></tr><tr><td>Overweight Hybrids vs. BBBs</td><td>We view the additional spread compensation of hybrids vs. BBBs as moderately attractive in the context of the tight spread environment. We also expect the hybrid spread premium to compress vs. BBBs, as the hybrid market grows further.</td></tr><tr><td rowspan="6">EUR market</td><td>Overweight IG vs. HY</td><td>We prefer IG over HY given the more challenging growth, inflation, and monetary policy mix in the Euro Area.</td></tr><tr><td>Overweight As vs. BBBs</td><td>In the EUR market, we prefer As over BBBs, as the weaker growth backdrop warrants more of an up-in-quality stance. That said, we would not move too far up the quality spectrum, as we expect supply technicals will be a headwind in the EUR AA market.</td></tr><tr><td>Overweight HY bonds vs. loans</td><td>Similar to the US, we prefer HY bonds given the loan market&#x27;s weighting toward software (and the potential negative sentiment overhang). The reaction function of the ECB may also increase borrowing costs for floating rate issuers, given the central bank&#x27;s focus on inflationary pressures.</td></tr><tr><td>Overweight BBs, Neutral Bs, Underweight CCCs</td><td>At current valuations and given our expectation for heightened dispersion, we prefer moving up in quality within EUR HY.</td></tr><tr><td>Underweight AT1s vs. EUR HY</td><td>Given downside tail risks and a limited valuation cushion, we recommend an underweight on AT1s vs. EUR HY.</td></tr><tr><td>Neutral Hybrids vs. BBBs</td><td>The additional carry vs. EUR BBBs is unlikely to compress further. Given our more cautious positioning in the EUR market (relative to USD), we would focus on higher-quality hybrid issuers in more economically resilient sectors.</td></tr></table>

Investors should review current options and futures disclosure documents which are available from GS sales representatives or at https://www.theocc.com/about/publications/character-risks.jsp and https://www.goldmansachs.com/disclosures/cftc\_fcm\_disclosures. Transaction costs may be significant in option strategies calling for multiple purchase and sales of options such as spreads. Supporting documentation will be supplied upon request.

Differing Levels of Service provided by Global Investment Research: The level and types of services provided to you by GS Global Investment Research may vary as compared to that provided to internal and other external clients of GS, depending on various factors including your individual preferences as to the frequency and manner of receiving communication, your risk profile and investment focus and perspective (e.g., marketwide, sector specific, long term, short term), the size and scope of your overall client relationship with GS, and legal and regulatory constraints. As an example, certain clients may request to receive notifications when research on specific securities is published, and certain clients may request that specific data underlying analysts' fundamental analysis available on our internal client websites be delivered to them electronically through data feeds or otherwise. No change to an analyst's fundamental research views (e.g., ratings, price targets, or material changes to earnings estimates for equity securities), will be communicated to any client prior to inclusion of such information in a research report broadly disseminated through electronic publication to our internal client websites or through other means, as necessary, to all clients who are entitled to receive such reports.

All research reports are disseminated and available to all clients simultaneously through electronic publication to our internal client websites. Not all research content is redistributed to our clients or available to third-party aggregators, nor is GS responsible for the redistribution of our research by third party aggregators. For research, models or other data related to one or more securities, markets or asset classes (including related services) that may be available to you, please contact your GS representative or go to https://research.gs.com.

Disclosure information is also available at https://www.gs.com/research/hedge.html or from Research Compliance, 200 West Street, New York, NY 10282.
